      "json_block": "{\"returncode\":0,\"stdout\":\"======================================================================\\nANALYZING: Slot end geometry - capsule vs filleted\\n======================================================================\\n\\nCurrent V1.2 make_slot_hole helper produces a CAPSULE shape:\\n  rect = Part.makeBox(slot_l - slot_w, slot_w, depth)\\n  cyl1 = Part.makeCylinder(slot_w/2, depth)  # left end cap\\n  cyl2 = Part.makeCylinder(slot_w/2, depth)  # right end cap\\n  slot = rect.fuse(cyl1).fuse(cyl2)\\n\\nThis creates:\\n\\n  ___________________\\n /                   \\\\    <- semicircular end (radius = slot_w/2)\\n|                     |   <- straight side\\n|                     |\\n \\\\___________________/   <- semicircular end\\n\\nSo the slot ENDS are already perfect semicircles (half-circles).\\nThere's no 'sharp corner' at the slot ends in this geometry.\\n\\nThe slot END semicircle radius = slot_w/2:\\n  For M5 through-hole slots: 5.5/2 = 2.75mm radius\\n  For M5 head pocket slots:  11/2 = 5.5mm radius\\n\\n======================================================================\\nSo what is the user asking for?\\n======================================================================\\n\\nPossibility 1: Sharp corners at the rectangle-cylinder junction?\\n  No - the cylinder fuses to the rectangle smoothly. No corners there.\\n\\nPossibility 2: Sharp edges where slot meets the foot top/bottom faces?\\n  YES - this is real. The slot has SHARP 90-degree edges where it\\n  intersects the top and bottom faces of the foot.\\n  These edges run AROUND the slot perimeter (capsule shape).\\n\\n  At the SLOT ENDS specifically (the semicircular parts), these\\n  90-degree edges are circular. They don't have geometric sharp\\n  corners, but they DO have sharp 90-degree transitions in the\\n  vertical direction (where slot wall meets foot face).\\n\\nPossibility 3: Stress concentration at slot end where load is applied?\\n  When bolt is tightened, the bolt SHANK pushes against the slot wall.\\n  Under belt tension, the bracket pulls and bolt presses the slot END.\\n  The slot end is curved (semicircle), but the EDGE between the slot\\n  wall and the foot top face is a sharp 90 degree corner.\\n\\n  Filleting that edge (in the vertical direction) creates a smooth\\n  transition that reduces stress concentration where the bolt loads\\n  the slot wall.\\n\\n======================================================================\\nTHE FIX: Vertical fillet at slot end-walls\\n======================================================================\\n\\nWhat we want:\\n  - Slot top edge: sharp 90 degree -> curved with radius (vertical)\\n  - Slot bottom edge: same (only on through-hole slot, not head pocket)\\n\\nImplementation options:\\n\\nOption A: makeFillet on slot edges\\n  - Same fragility issues as before\\n  - Hard to filter just the slot edges without catching others\\n\\nOption B: Modify make_slot_hole to produce naturally rounded vertical edges\\n  - Use a sweep or revolve operation\\n  - More complex but more reliable\\n\\nOption C: Add explicit small radius cylinders at slot ENDS\\n  - Take the existing semi-circular end caps\\n  - Make them slightly smaller in radius at the TOP of the foot\\n  - This creates a chamfer-like transition at slot ends\\n  - Only at the load-bearing ends, not along the straight sides\\n\\nHmm, looking at this more carefully...\\n\\nActually the user said 'radius to slot ends' specifically.\\nThe slot ENDS are already semicircular (capsule shape).\\n\\nLet me re-read the request: 'Add 0.5-1.0mm radius to slot ends\\n(explicitly modeled, not just capsule math)'\\n\\n'Not just capsule math' - this suggests they want EXTRA radius\\nbeyond the inherent capsule semi-circles.\\n\\nWhere exactly? I think they mean the VERTICAL EDGES where the slot\\nwall meets the foot top face. Specifically at the slot END ZONES\\n(the semicircular parts) which are the highest-stress regions.\\n\\nImplementation:\\n  Make the slot wall slightly TAPER inward at the top of the foot.\\n  This creates a small 'bevel' or 'fillet-like' transition at the\\n  slot end walls only, where the bolt presses under load.\\n\\nGeometric approach: at each slot END, subtract a TORUS-shaped relief\\nor a small cylinder with rounded top edge.\\n\\nSimpler approach: sweep a profile along the slot end semicircles\\nthat has a small chamfer at the top.\\n\\nEven simpler approach: at each slot end (semicircular), add a small\\nDOMED cylinder that creates a beveled top edge.\\n\\n======================================================================\\nPRACTICAL IMPLEMENTATION\\n======================================================================\\n\\nI'll modify the make_slot_hole helper to produce a slot whose end\\nsemicircles have a small CHAMFERED TOP EDGE. This adds ~0.5-1mm\\nradius/chamfer at the slot ends specifically.\\n\\nMethod:\\n  1. Create the standard capsule slot (current behavior)\\n  2. At each slot END center, subtract a 90-degree chamfer cone\\n     - Cone matches semicircle radius at top of foot\\n     - Cone tapers inward as it goes down by the chamfer amount\\n     - This rounds the top edge of the slot end region\\n\\nThis addresses crack initiation at slot ends specifically, where\\nthe bolt repeatedly presses during tightening cycles.\\n\\nMath:\\n  M5 slot end radius: 2.75mm (half of slot width 5.5mm)\\n  Chamfer 0.8mm: cone goes from r=2.75 at top to r=2.0 at 0.8mm down\\n  This creates a 0.8mm chamfer on the slot end perimeter\\n\\nCode outline:\\n\\ndef make_slot_hole_with_relief(slot_w, slot_l, depth, end_relief=0.8):\\n    # Standard capsule slot\\n    radius = slot_w / 2.0\\n    rect_l = slot_l - slot_w\\n    rect = Part.makeBox(rect_l, slot_w, depth)\\n    rect.translate(FreeCAD.Vector(-rect_l/2.0, -slot_w/2.0, 0))\\n    cyl1 = Part.makeCylinder(radius, depth)\\n    cyl1.translate(FreeCAD.Vector(-rect_l/2.0, 0, 0))\\n    cyl2 = Part.makeCylinder(radius, depth)\\n    cyl2.translate(FreeCAD.Vector(rect_l/2.0, 0, 0))\\n    slot = rect.fuse(cyl1).fuse(cyl2)\\n\\n    # Add chamfer cones at slot ends (top of slot only)\\n    if end_relief > 0:\\n        for x_pos in [-rect_l/2.0, rect_l/2.0]:\\n            # Cone goes from full radius at top to (radius - end_relief) below\\n            cone = Part.makeCone(radius + 0.1,\\n                                 radius - end_relief,\\n                                 end_relief)\\n            cone.translate(FreeCAD.Vector(x_pos, 0, depth - end_relief))\\n            slot = slot.fuse(cone)\\n    return slot\\n\\n\\nWait - I'm subtracting from a hole. The hole is the CUT shape.\\nIf I FUSE a cone to the hole, the cone EXPANDS the hole at the top.\\nThat means more material is removed at the top -> chamfered upper edge.\\n\\nActually I want the OPPOSITE: chamfer the TOP edge of the hole.\\nTo chamfer the top edge OUTWARD: hole gets WIDER at the top.\\nThat means the hole shape itself needs to be a flared cylinder near the top.\\n\\nYes - the cone fused to the hole gives a wider opening at top = chamfered edge.\\nThis is correct.\\n\",\"stderr\":\"\"}"

Added a `direction` parameter.\n\n3. **Final state:** Macro applies relief at **two** critical edges per slot \u2014 foot bottom (T-nut bearing edge) and head pocket transition. There are **two** stress-concentration sites at each slot, not one:\n\n**1. Foot bottom face (Z = -10mm)** \u2014 where the T-nut bears against the slot edge under belt tension load. Highest stress location. Chamfer flares **downward** (out of bottom face).\n\n**2. Head pocket transition (Z = -5mm)** \u2014 where the geometry steps from D5.5 through-hole to D11 head pocket, AND where bolt shank loads slot wall. Chamfer flares **upward** (into head pocket).\n\nBoth reliefs are at the slot END semicircles (where the bolt presses), not along the straight sides. 0.8mm chamfer size \u2014 within your suggested 0.5-1.0mm range.\n\n## Implementation note\n\nI introduced a new helper function `add_slot_end_relief()` with explicit `direction` parameter (up/down) for proper chamfer orientation. This is more reliable than `makeFillet` on complex fused geometry, which has been a recurring failure point in earlier review cycles.\n\n## What I rejected from the review\n\n| Point | Verdict |\n|---|---|\n| L-corner fillet via makeFillet | REJECTED \u2014 filter too broad, would catch slot/counterbore/perimeter edges |\n| Shift rib base another 2mm | REJECTED \u2014 diminishing returns, eats into plate body |\n| CHAMFER_SIZE 1.0 \u2192 0.6mm | REJECTED \u2014 chamfers not in load path, no real benefit |\n| \"Rotate so ribs grow upward\" | NON-ISSUE \u2014 already correct with foot DOWN orientation |\n\n## Honest debugging note\n\nBuilding this caught a real bug in my own code. My first attempt placed the relief cone in the slot's local Z coordinates, which after translation put the cone **above** the foot in air (doing nothing useful). I caught this by tracing the math and refactored to use world coordinates with explicit Z heights.\n\nThis is the kind of bug that wouldn't show up until somebody tried to print and noticed sharp slot edges \u2014 exactly what you said V1.3 was supposed to fix. Glad I caught it before delivering.\n\n## Drive system status \u2014 DESIGN COMPLETE\n\n| Part | Version | Status |\n|---|---|---|\n| Cam ring | V5 | Print-ready |\n| Bearing housings (top + bottom) | V2.4 | Print-ready (deterministic preload) |\n| Spacer tube | V2.4 | Print-ready (29.40mm) |\n| Drive hub | V1.3 | Print-ready (production-perfect) |\n| **Motor mount** | **V1.3** | **Print-ready (this delivery, durability-refined)** |\n\n**The entire drive subsystem is now design-locked.** Five parts.       "json_block": "{\"returncode\":0,\"stdout\":\"======================================================================\\nV2.4 BEARING HOUSING - SPRING POCKET CONSTRAINTS\\n======================================================================\\n\\nLooking back at V2.4 bearing housing geometry to see what actually\\nconstrains the wave spring dimensions...\\n\\nV2.4 SPRING POCKET SPEC (from prior session):\\n  Wave spring pocket: D26.3mm x 1.4mm deep\\n  Spacer ring pocket: D26.3mm x 2.0mm deep\\n\\nSo the BEARING HOUSING was designed with a 26.3mm diameter pocket.\\nThis means anything with OD <= 26.3mm fits.\\n\\nBEARING SPEC: 6001-2RS\\n  Inner: 12mm\\n  Outer: 28mm\\n  Width: 8mm\\n\\nSo the ACTUAL constraints are:\\n  Inner: must be > 12mm (clears shaft)\\n  Outer: must be <= 26.3mm (fits in pocket)\\n  Material: needs to sit on the bearing inner race or spacer ring\\n\\n======================================================================\\nWAVE SPRING vs BELLEVILLE - which load profile do we want?\\n======================================================================\\n\\nOriginal V2.4 design: 30N preload via wave spring\\n\\nWave spring characteristics:\\n  - Linear-ish load curve\\n  - Compresses smoothly\\n  - Maintains preload over service life\\n  - Standard for bearing preload applications\\n\\nBelleville (disc) spring characteristics:\\n  - Highly nonlinear S-curve\\n  - Can be stacked in various configurations\\n  - Higher load capacity per height\\n  - Common in automotive/industrial preload\\n  - HARDER to predict exact preload force\\n\\nStacked wave springs:\\n  - 2x lighter springs in series\\n  - Linear behavior preserved\\n  - Tall stack height\\n\\n======================================================================\\nWHAT'S ACTUALLY AVAILABLE - LET'S CHECK CATALOG SIZES\\n======================================================================\\n\\nSmalley standard CB series (C-Crest, single turn):\\n  Common 12mm ID parts:\\n  - CB-12: 12 ID x 17.27 OD x 0.25t, F = 23N at flat\\n  - SSB-12: 12 ID x 19 OD x 0.30t, F = various\\n\\nNote: standard Smalley 12mm ID parts have ~17-19mm OD, not 25mm\\n\\nMcMaster-Carr standard wave washers:\\n  Inch sizes dominate. Metric 12mm ID is uncommon.\\n\\nBelleville washers (DIN 2093):\\n  Standard sizes for 12mm shaft:\\n  - 25mm OD x 0.9mm thick - F at 0.4mm = ~370N (TOO STIFF)\\n  - 23mm OD x 0.6mm thick - F at 0.3mm = ~140N (too stiff)\\n  - 20mm OD x 0.5mm thick - F at 0.4mm = ~70N (still stiff)\\n\\nHmm. Belleville disc springs at 12mm ID are mostly too stiff for 30N.\\n\\n======================================================================\\nWHAT IF WE ACTUALLY DON'T NEED 30N?\\n======================================================================\\n\\nGoing back to engineering basics: WHY 30N?\\n\\nBearing preload purpose:\\n  - Eliminate axial play (clicks, rattles)\\n  - Maintain consistent bearing position under varying loads\\n  - Compensate for thermal expansion\\n\\nFor 6001-2RS bearings (deep groove ball, light duty):\\n  Manufacturer recommended preload: 0-50N typical\\n  30N was a middle-of-range choice\\n\\nReality check: 30N is NOT a hard requirement.\\nAnything in the 10-50N range will work.\\n\\nThis OPENS UP the spring options significantly.\\n\\n======================================================================\\nRECOMMENDED ALTERNATIVE - STANDARD SMALLEY CB-12\\n======================================================================\\n\\nSmalley part: CB-12-S (or similar standard catalog number)\\n  Verify part numbers via web search\\n  - Inner: 12mm\\n  - Outer: ~17-19mm\\n  - Material: 17-7PH stainless\\n  - Load: ~15-25N typical\\n  - Single turn, 3-wave\\n\\nIssue: OD only 17-19mm vs pocket 26.3mm\\nThis means the spring SITS LOOSE in the pocket (3-4mm radial slop)\\n\\nIs that a problem?\\n\\nThe spring's job is axial compression. Radial position doesn't matter\\nAS LONG AS it stays centered enough to push axially without binding.\\n\\nWith 3-4mm radial play, the spring CAN drift sideways.\\nSolution: smaller spring pocket OR centering feature.\\n\\nCleanest fix: REDUCE the pocket OD in V2.5 bearing housings\\n\\n  - Change pocket from D26.3 to D19.5 (matches Smalley ~19mm OD)\\n  - 0.5mm radial clearance\\n  - Spring sits centered in smaller pocket\\n\\nTrade-off: 6001 bearing OD is 28mm, so the housing pocket can't be\\nsmaller than 28mm where the bearing sits.\\nBUT the SPRING pocket is BELOW the bearing pocket - it can be smaller.\\n\\nThis means a STEPPED pocket geometry:\\n  Bearing seat: D28 x 8mm deep (full bearing OD)\\n  Spring pocket: D19.5 x 1.4mm deep (smaller, just for spring)\\n  Spacer pocket: D19.5 x 2.0mm deep (smaller, just for spacer)\\n\\nThis requires updating V2.4 -> V2.5 bearing housing macros.\\n\",\"stderr\":\"\"}"

        "text": "For Overlap Type Wave Springs and multiple turn Spirawaves, the radial wall must be sufficient to prevent misalignment between adjacent layers. For springs with a narrow radial wall, radial misalignment can occur during handling or during operation if the spring is not contained or closely piloted. Solutions to this problem include dimensioning the spring to pilot closely on the I.D. and/or O.D. or designing the spring as a single turn Gap Type. For springs that pilot on a shaft, the inside diameter can be toleranced to provide a minimum clearance from the shaft. Since wave springs expand during compression, interference with the shaft is generally not a concern. For springs that pilot in the bore, the bore and shaft diameters should be included in the spring specifications. The actual spring diameter is then developed at time of manufacture to provide the best fit and prevent binding due to expansion.\n\nStandard Springs vs Custom Springs Finding the right spring can be as easy as selecting a standard catalog item. A Smalley engineer can help you choose from over 4000 standard parts available from stock in carbon and stainless steel. Smalley\u2019s edgewinding manufacturing provides the utmost in flexibility and quality. Whether the requirement is for one spring or one million, consider Smalley for your special spring requirements. Defining the Spring Requirements Although wave spring applications are extremely diverse, there is a consistently basic set of rules for defining spring requirements.\n\nAlthough wave spring applications are extremely diverse, there is a consistently basic set of rules for defining spring requirements. Those requirements are used to select a stock/standard spring or design a special spring to meet the specifications. The working cavity usually consists of a bore the spring operates in and/or a shaft the spring clears. The spring stays positioned by piloting in the bore or on the shaft. The distance between the loading surfaces defines the axial working cavity or work height of the spring. Material cross-section plays an important role in wave spring design. The most economical materials are those used in manufacturing Smalley standard springs and retaining rings. In addition, many other material cross sections are commonly used in special spring manufacture designs. Smalley engineering can provide assistance in selecting an economical alloy and cross section.\n\nFor Overlap Type Wave Springs and multiple turn Spirawaves, the radial wall must be sufficient to prevent misalignment between adjacent layers.         "text": "Conventional Gap and Overlap Type Wave Springs are used in a wide variety of applications. For short deflections and low-medium forces, they function with precision and dependability. These two types of Smalley Wave Springs permit radial expansion or growth in diameter within a cavity, without the binding or hang-up normally associated with die stamped wave washers. Just as their terms imply, the gap type is split to retain a gap between the ends; while the overlap type has overlapping ends. Thus, the ends are free to move circumferentially as the spring outside diameter grows during compression. For example, the O.D. of a Gap Type Wave Spring would fit .020 loose per side in a bore. Its I.D. clears a shaft by .010 per side. As the spring is deflected, the O.D. and I.D. grow larger until the O.D. contacts the bore. Continued deflection causes the gap ends to move closer together ...\n\nMaterial: Stainless Steel; High Carbon Steel; Coil Shape: Straight; Spring Pitch: Constant Pitch; Base Unit System: English Design; Outer Diameter: 2.25 inch (57.15 mm); Inner Diameter: 1.93 inch (49.02 mm); Wire diameter: 0.1500 inch (3.81 mm); Free length: 0.1700 inch (4.32 mm); Solid height: 0.0930 inch (2.36 mm); Spring rate: 494 lbs/in (865 N/cm);",

When assembling individual bearing components, the outer race, inner race, balls, and retainer all work together so that there is a controlled amount of internal clearance between the rings and balls.     "text": "Now I have a clear picture. Here's my honest assessment:\n\n## You're right \u2014 the original V2.4 wave spring spec was unworkable\n\nThe 12mm ID \u00d7 25mm OD \u00d7 0.45mm thick \u00d7 30N at 0.5mm is **not a catalog item anywhere**. I shouldn't have specified it as if it were standard. That was a real mistake from earlier in the project.\n\n## What real Smalley CM25 parts look like\n\nI verified directly from Smalley's site:\n\n| Part | Bore (Db) | Shaft (Ds) | Load | Free Height | Turns |\n|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n| CM25-L1 | 25mm | 19mm | 50N | 6.63mm | 3 |\n| CM25-M1 | 25mm | 19mm | 80N | 6.63mm | 3 |\n| CM25-H1 | 25mm | 19mm | 110N | 6.63mm | 3 |\n\n**Key issue:** All standard CM25 parts have **6.63mm free height** (multi-turn). My V2.4 housing pocket is only **1.4mm deep**. The spring won't even fit before compression.\n\n## Three paths forward\n\n**A. Redesign V2.4 \u2192 V2.5 bearing housings**\n- Spring pocket 1.4mm \u2192 ~5.5mm deep\n- Recalculate spacer tube length (currently 29.40mm)\n- Use real Smalley CM25-L1 (~$5-10 each)\n- Means redesigning the housing macro AND recomputing the preload math\n\n**B. Eliminate the wave spring entirely** \u2190 my recommendation\n- V2.4 already has **deterministic preload via the spacer tube**\n- The wave spring was effectively \"belt and suspenders\"\n- Simpler design, fewer parts to source\n- One real concern: thermal expansion compensation (~0.054mm over 30\u00b0C range)\n\n**C. Use Belleville (disc) spring**\n- Standard DIN 2093 sizes available at McMaster\n- 12mm ID parts exist but most are too stiff for our load\n- Different load curve (nonlinear)\n- Less ideal for bearing preload than wave springs\n\n## Why I recommend B (eliminate spring)\n\nThe V2.4 design philosophy was **deterministic preload via spacer tube length**. The math works out to 30N at 0.5mm compression based on bearing inner-race position. The wave spring was added as a \"soft\" backup in case the spacer tube was the wrong length.\n\nBut:\n1. We already designed the spacer tube length precisely (29.40mm)\n2. We can verify it with a dial indicator before final assembly\n3. PETG thermal expansion over 30\u00b0C is only 0.054mm \u2014 small enough that the bearings absorb it via internal clearance\n4. Removing the spring eliminates a sourcing problem AND a potentially-incorrect part\n5. Bearings work fine without springs in many designs (machine tool spindles use shims, not springs)\n\n## What this looks like in practice\n\nIf you go with option B:\n\n- **V2.4 bearing housings**: keep as-is (the spring pocket can stay; just don't put a spring in it, or design V2.5 without the pocket)\n- **Spacer tube**: keep at 29.40mm\n- **No wave spring needed**: -$10 from BOM\n- **No spacer ring needed**: -$10 from BOM\n- **Net savings**: $20 + sourcing headache\n\n## My ask\n\nBefore I go further, which path do you want?\n\n1. **Option B** (eliminate spring, keep V2.4 housings as-is) \u2014 fastest, cleanest\n2. **Option A** (redesign to V2.5 with proper pocket for CM25-L1) \u2014 uses real catalog part, but means another macro iteration\n3. **Option C** (Belleville stack) \u2014 different load curve, requires research\n\nOr another option I haven't considered. Your call.",
    "citations": []
  }
]

    "text": "I worked out the math but I want to flag something important before writing the macro:\n\n## Critical issue I'm flagging before building V2.5\n\nThe original V2.4 design relied on **deterministic preload via geometric spacer tube length**. The 0.5mm compression target was based on a notional spring with 0.45mm thickness \u2014 basically a wave washer that essentially \"snapped flat\" when the right gap was achieved.\n\n**With CM25-L1 the dynamics are completely different:**\n\n- Free height: 6.63mm\n- 30N preload requires 2.74mm compression (not 0.5mm)\n- This is 5\u00d7 more compression travel\n- Spring rate: 10.94 N/mm\n\n**This changes the design philosophy.** Instead of \"deterministic preload via tight spacer tube tolerance\", the spring now provides \"spring-loaded preload via controlled compression range.\" Both work, but the second is more forgiving of tolerance variation \u2014 small variations in spacer tube length result in small variations in preload, not on/off behavior.\n\n**Tolerance sensitivity comparison:**\n- V2.4 (0.45mm spring): 0.1mm tolerance error \u2192 \u00b120-50% preload variation\n- V2.5 (CM25-L1): 0.1mm tolerance error \u2192 \u00b11.1N preload variation (3.6% of 30N)\n\nThis is actually a **significant engineering improvement**, not just a parts substitution.\n\n## Decision points before I write the macro\n\n1. **Spacer tube length:** 28.36mm (recalculated). Need 18mm OD aluminum tube cut to this length. If you've already cut a 29.40mm tube, you'd need a new one.\n\n2. **Spacer ring:** Spec changes from (12 ID \u00d7 18 OD \u00d7 2mm) to (19.5 ID \u00d7 25.5 OD \u00d7 1.0mm). McMaster 92141A050 (19 ID \u00d7 28 OD \u00d7 1.6mm steel washer) is close enough \u2014 bigger OD doesn't matter since spring sits inside the pocket.\n\n3. **Block height:** 35mm in V2.4. Two options:\n   - **A:** Keep at 35mm, just move bearing seat 0.6mm deeper into block\n   - **B:** Increase to 35.6mm to preserve same wall thickness above bearing\n   \n   I recommend **A** (keep 35mm) \u2014 simpler, no impact on outer geometry.\n\n4. **Versioning:** Bearing housings stay on the V2.x line. This is V2.5.\n\n5. **Hardware impact for your existing orders:**\n   - You have NOT yet ordered the wave spring (was deferred)\n   - You have NOT yet ordered the spacer ring (was deferred)\n   - You have NOT yet cut the spacer tube (was waiting on aluminum stock)\n   - **So no waste from prior orders** \u2014 V2.5 is a clean redesign\n\n## My honest concern\n\n**Should we keep the spacer tube at all?**\n\nThe spacer tube was V2.4's deterministic preload mechanism. With CM25-L1, the spring itself controls preload \u2014 the tube just needs to position the bearings the right distance apart, with enough compression range left in the spring.\n\nThe spacer tube is now just a \"minimum gap setter,\" not a precision-tuned preload component. We could also use **2 shaft collars** to set bearing positions, eliminating the spacer tube entirely.\n\nBut the tube has advantages:\n- Can't slip on the shaft\n- Single-piece sets exact distance\n- Aluminum tube is cheap\n\nI'd keep the spacer tube but note that it's now less critical to \u00b10.05mm precision.\n\n## Question before I build\n\nAre these decisions OK?\n- **Pocket: D25.6 \u00d7 4.0mm**\n- **Spacer ring: M5 washer-style, 19.5 ID \u00d7 25.5 OD \u00d7 1.0mm**\n- **Block height: keep 35mm**\n- **Spacer tube length: 28.36mm**\n- **Target preload: 30N (CM25-L1 at 2.74mm compression)**\n- **Both top + bottom housings updated**\n\nOr do you want to adjust any of these before I write the macro? Specifically:\n- Higher preload (use CM25-M1 at 80N spec)?\n- Lower preload (CM25-L1 at less compression)?\n- Different spring (CM25-L3 with more turns, lower spring rate)?\n\nJust confirm the parameters and I'll build V2.5.",

SAFETY ANALYSIS\")\nprint(\"=\" * 70)\nprint()\nprint(\"For each preload target, calculate compression and safety margin:\")\nprint()\ntargets = [30, 35, 40, 45, 50]\nfree_h = 6.63\nwork_h = 2.06\nrate = 10.94\n\nfor F in targets:\n    compression = F / rate\n    operating_h = free_h - compression\n    margin = operating_h - work_h\n    pct_of_max = (F / 50.0) * 100\n    \n    print(f\"  {F}N preload:\")\n    print(f\"    Compression:    {compression:.2f} mm\")\n    print(f\"    Operating H:    {operating_h:.2f} mm\")\n    print(f\"    Margin to WH:   {margin:.2f} mm ({pct_of_max:.0f}% of max load)\")\n    print()\n\nprint(\"=\" * 70)\nprint(\"REVIEWER'S 40N RECOMMENDATION ANALYSIS\")\nprint(\"=\" * 70)\nprint()\nprint(\"At 40N:\")\nprint(\"  Compression: 3.66 mm\")\nprint(\"  Operating height: 2.97 mm\")\nprint(\"  Margin to work height: 0.91 mm (80% of max load)\")\nprint()\nprint(\"Concerns:\")\nprint(\"  - Only 0.91mm margin before overstress\")\nprint(\"  - Tolerance stack of +/- 0.3mm could push us close to limit\")\nprint(\"  - Thermal expansion (~0.05mm) reduces margin further\")\nprint(\"  - 80% of rated load is high for long service life\")\nprint()\nprint(\"My assessment: 40N is VALID but tight.\")\nprint()\nprint(\"Better engineering choice: 35N\")\nprint(\"  Compression: 3.20 mm\")\nprint(\"  Operating H: 3.43 mm\")\nprint(\"  Margin to WH: 1.37 mm (70% of max load)\")\nprint()\nprint(\"This gives:\")\nprint(\"  - 1.37mm safety margin (vs 0.91mm at 40N)\")\nprint(\"  - 70% of max load (sustainable long-term)\")\nprint(\"  - Splits difference between 30N (too low) and 50N (too risky)\")\nprint(\"  - Still much better than V2.4's 30N target\")\nprint()\nprint(\"=\" * 70)\nprint(\"USE CM25-L2 INSTEAD?\")\nprint(\"=\" * 70)\nprint()\nprint(\"CM25-L2 specs (from search):\")\nprint(\"  Db: 25mm, Ds: 19mm\")\nprint(\"  Load at WH: 50N\")\nprint(\"  Work height: 2.74mm  <-- DIFFERENT from L1's 2.06mm\")\nprint(\"  Free height: ~9mm (more turns)\")\nprint()\nprint(\"CM25-L2 has MORE compression range than L1.\")\nprint(\"  More turns = lower spring rate, longer free height, same max load\")\nprint()\nprint(\"Honestly, L1 is fine. Don't overcomplicate.\")\nprint()\nprint(\"=\" * 70)\nprint(\"FINAL DECISION\")\nprint(\"=\" * 70)\nprint()\nprint(\"Spring: CM25-L1 (3 turns, 17-7 SS)\")\nprint(\"Target preload: 35N\")\nprint(\"  Reviewer wanted 40N - I'll go 35N for better safety margin\")\nprint(\"  Difference is small (15% lower load)\")\nprint(\"  The reviewer's concern about 30N being too low is valid\")\nprint(\"  But 40N pushes too close to overstress limit\")\nprint(\"  35N is the engineering sweet spot\")\nprint()\nprint(\"Compression at 35N: 3.20mm\")\nprint(\"Operating height: 3.43mm\")\nprint()\nprint(\"=\" * 70)\nprint(\"V2.5 GEOMETRY - FINAL\")\nprint(\"=\" * 70)\nprint()\nPLATE_THICK = 6.0  # Al plate\nSPRING_FREE = 6.63\nSPRING_OPERATING = 3.43  # at 35N\nTARGET_PRELOAD = 35.0\n\n# Spacer ring: McMaster 92141A050 = 19 ID x 28 OD x 1.6mm\nWASHER_T = 1.6\nprint(f\"Pocket: D25.7 x 4.0mm (was 25.6mm, +0.1 radial clearance)\")\nprint(f\"Spring at 35N: 3.43mm height\")\nprint(f\"Spacer washer: {WASHER_T}mm thick (McMaster 92141A050)\")\nprint(f\"  Stack in pocket: 3.43 + 1.6 = 5.03mm\")\nprint(f\"  Pocket depth: 4.0mm\")\nprint(f\"  WAIT - stack 5.03 > pocket 4.0!\")\nprint(f\"  Spring + washer is TALLER than pocket!\")\nprint()\nprint(\"This is intentional - spring extends above pocket, gets compressed\")\nprint(\"by the bearing pressing down. Pocket bottom is the floor that\")\nprint(\"spring sits on. Bearing comes from above and compresses spring.\")\nprint()\nprint(\"Re-thinking pocket depth:\")\nprint(\"  Spring at preload: 3.43mm\")\nprint(\"  + Spacer washer:   1.6mm\")\nprint(\"  Total at preload:  5.03mm of height needed\")\nprint()\nprint(\"If pocket depth = 4.0mm: stack extends 1.03mm above pocket\")\nprint(\"That extension is INTO the bearing seat (where bearing sits)\")\nprint(\"Spring/washer pushes bearing UP by 1.03mm? NO - that's the design.\")\nprint()\nprint(\"Actually, here's the real geometry:\")\nprint(\"  Pocket bottom = solid surface (spring rests here)\")\nprint(\"  Pocket walls = 4.0mm tall guides\")\nprint(\"  Bearing seat = above pocket, larger diameter\")\nprint(\"  Bearing rests on... the TOP of the spacer washer (which is above pocket)\")\nprint()\nprint(\"So bearing seat must START at pocket_bottom + spring_at_preload + washer\")\nprint(\"  = 0 + 3.43 + 1.6 = 5.03mm above pocket bottom\")\nprint()\nprint(\"V2.5 cavity (top housing):\")\nprint(\"  Pocket bottom: Z = 0 (reference)\")\nprint(\"  Pocket walls (D25.7) extend from Z=0 to Z=4.0mm\")\nprint(\"  Bearing seat (D28.05) starts at Z = 5.03mm (after spring + washer)\")\nprint(\"  WAIT - that leaves a 1.03mm gap with no walls?\")\nprint()\nprint(\"Better design: pocket walls match spring+washer stack height\")\nprint(\"  Pocket walls D25.7 from Z=0 to Z=5.03mm (or slightly less)\")\nprint(\"  Spring extends 3.43mm + washer 1.6mm = 5.03mm at preload\")\nprint(\"  Bearing seat D28.05 starts at Z=5.03mm\")\nprint()\nprint(\"BUT: when spring is at FREE HEIGHT (6.63mm), it sticks UP into bearing seat\")\nprint(\"  6.63 > 4.0 walls, so spring is partially in seat area\")\nprint(\"  That's OK - spring guides itself on washer, doesn't bind\")\nprint()\nprint(\"Cleanest solution: pocket depth 5.5mm (covers max preload stack + clearance)\")\nprint(\"  At preload (35N): 3.43 + 1.6 = 5.03 of 5.5 used (good)\")\nprint(\"  At max (50N):     2.06 + 1.6 = 3.66 of 5.5 used (lots of margin)\")\nprint(\"  Pocket walls fully guide spring throughout compression range\")\nprint()\nprint(\"FINAL POCKET DEPTH: 5.5mm\")\nprint()\nprint(\"V2.4 cavity depth: 11.4mm (1.4 + 2.0 + 8.0)\")\nprint(\"V2.5 cavity depth: 13.5mm (5.5 + 8.0)\")\nprint(\"Difference: 2.1mm deeper cavity per side\")\nprint()\nprint(\"=\" * 70)\nprint(\"SPACER TUBE LENGTH RECALCULATION\")\nprint(\"=\" * 70)\nprint()\nprint(\"V2.4 bearing-to-bearing: 30.3mm (legacy design)\")\nprint()\nprint(\"V2.5 changes:\")\nprint(\"  Each side: cavity 2.1mm deeper than V2.4\")\nprint(\"  Both sides: 4.2mm more bearing-to-bearing? NO - bearings move\")\nprint(\"  AWAY from the housing center, but the housing centers don't change.\")\nprint()\nprint(\"Hmm, actually V2.4 bearing-to-bearing was a fixed geometry.\")\nprint(\"If we make pockets deeper, bearings sit further from each other.\")\nprint()\nprint(\"V2.5 nominal bearing-to-bearing: 30.3 + 4.2 = 34.5mm? That's a lot.\")\nprint()\nprint(\"Wait. Let me think about it from bearing position.\")\nprint()\nprint(\"Block has aluminum plate sandwiched between top + bottom blocks.\")\nprint(\"  Plate is at Z = 0 (reference)\")\nprint(\"  Top block sits above plate, ranges Z = 0 to +35\")\nprint(\"  Bottom block hangs below plate, ranges Z = 0 to -35\")\nprint()\nprint(\"Bearings in pockets:\")\nprint(\"  V2.4: bearing center at Z = +(35 - 11.4 + 4) = +27.6 (top), -27.6 (bottom)\")\nprint(\"    Actually wait, cavity opens AWAY from plate. Bearing in pocket nearest to plate.\")\nprint()\nprint(\"Argh, I'm confusing myself. Let me just do the relative math.\")\nprint()\nprint(\"V2.5 vs V2.4: cavity is 2.1mm deeper.\")\nprint(\"If cavity opens TOWARD plate (mating face), bearings sit 2.1mm\")\nprint(\"  FURTHER FROM plate -> bearings sit 2.1mm FARTHER from each other.\")\nprint(\"If cavity opens AWAY FROM plate, bearings sit 2.1mm\")\nprint(\"  CLOSER TO plate -> bearings sit 2.1mm CLOSER together.\")\nprint()\nprint(\"V2.4 design: cavity opens TOWARD plate (so spring is between plate and bearing)\")\nprint(\"Therefore deeper V2.5 cavity = bearings 2.1mm FURTHER apart per side\")\nprint(\"Both sides: bearings 4.2mm FURTHER apart\")\nprint()\nprint(\"V2.5 nominal bearing-to-bearing: 30.3 + 4.2 = 34.5mm\")\nprint()\nprint(\"V2.5 spacer tube length:\")\nprint(\"  34.5 - 3.20 (compression) - 0.5 (tolerance) = 30.80mm\")\nprint()\nprint(\"Round to: 30.8mm\")\nprint()\nprint(\"=\" * 70)\nprint(\"V2.5 FINAL SPECS\")\nprint(\"=\" * 70)\nprint()\nprint(\"BEARING HOUSING (top + bottom, identical):\")\nprint(\"  Block: 80x80x35mm (unchanged from V2.4)\")\nprint(\"  Cavity depth: 13.5mm (was 11.4mm)\")\nprint(\"  Pocket: D25.7 x 5.5mm deep (was D26.3 x 1.4mm + D26.3 x 2.0mm)\")\nprint(\"  Bearing seat: D28.05 x 8mm (unchanged)\")\nprint(\"  Outer chamfer, slip fit + Loctite 603, M5 mount holes (all unchanged)\")\nprint()\nprint(\"SPACER TUBE:\")\nprint(\"  Length: 30.8mm (was 29.40mm)\")\nprint(\"  OD: 18mm (unchanged -     "text": "## Summary before I write V2.5 macro\n\nI worked through the math carefully. Important findings:\n\n**1. The reviewer was right that 30N is too low, but their 40N is too aggressive:**\n\n| Preload | Compression | Operating H | Margin to Work H | % of Max Load |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| 30N (V2.4 target) | 2.74mm | 3.89mm | 1.83mm | 60% |\n| **35N (proposed)** | **3.20mm** | **3.43mm** | **1.37mm** | **70%** |\n| 40N (reviewer ask) | 3.66mm | 2.97mm | 0.91mm | 80% |\n| 50N (max) | 4.57mm | 2.06mm | 0.00mm | 100% |\n\n**My recommendation: 35N target.** Splits the difference. 1.37mm safety margin handles tolerance + thermal expansion comfortably. 70% of max load is sustainable long-term.\n\n**2. Pocket depth needs to be 5.5mm, not 4.0mm.** The spacer washer is 1.6mm thick. Spring at 35N is 3.43mm. Stack = 5.03mm. Pocket walls need to fully guide the spring through its compression range \u2014 5.5mm gives clearance.\n\n**3. Cavity depth changes by 2.1mm (1.4+2.0=3.4 \u2192 5.5).** This makes bearing-to-bearing distance 4.2mm longer (2.1mm per side).\n\n**4. Spacer tube length: 30.8mm** (was 29.40mm). Per reviewer guidance, I'm rounding to 30.8mm rather than computing to 0.01mm precision \u2014 the new geometry is forgiving.\n\n## Final V2.5 specs (to be implemented)\n\n**Bearing housing (top + bottom, identical):**\n- Block: 80\u00d780\u00d735mm (unchanged)\n- Pocket: **D25.7 \u00d7 5.5mm deep** (was D26.3 \u00d7 1.4mm + spacer pocket)\n- Bearing seat: D28.05 \u00d7 8mm (unchanged)\n- Cavity depth: 13.5mm (was 11.4mm)\n\n**Spacer tube:**\n- Length: **30.8mm** (was 29.40mm)\n- OD/ID unchanged\n\n**Hardware:**\n- 2\u00d7 Smalley CM25-L1 wave springs (17-7 SS) \u2014 ~$8 each\n- 2\u00d7 spacer washers, McMaster 92141A050 (19 ID \u00d7 28 OD \u00d7 1.6mm) \u2014 ~$1 each\n\n**Preload:** 35N at 3.20mm compression, 1.37mm margin to work height\n\n## Reviewer points addressed\n\n| Point | My response |\n|---|---|\n| 40N preload | **Going with 35N** \u2014 safer margin, real engineering reason |\n| Verify solid height | **DONE** \u2014 confirmed via Smalley blog: work height = max safe |\n| Spacer tube \u00b10.5mm acceptable | **AGREED** \u2014 rounded to 30.8mm |\n| Pocket +0.1mm radial clearance | **APPLIED** \u2014 D25.6 \u2192 D25.7 |\n| Compensate washer thickness in tube length | **APPLIED** \u2014 calc includes 1.6mm washer |\n| Hard stop safety margin | **1.37mm safety margin** at 35N |\n\n## Question before I build\n\n**Are these final specs OK?** Specifically the **35N preload vs reviewer's 40N**.
